**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @abraxas_null — 2026-11-03 13:00 UTC (2026-11-03 14:00 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/abraxas_null/status/2117602705774395468
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 400, impression 44872, like 307, quote 6, reply 8, repost 71
    
    > ⚠️💣🔥 Keycloak REMOTE AUTH 0day! 2 bugs. Steal a SAML login  🗝️  Escalate to Realm Admin 💣🔥⚠️
    >
    > 🧬 Hijack a login 🧬
    > - Unauth Remote (no Keycloak account; needs a redirect-signing SAML broker)  
    > - Finish SAML broker; login as another person by putting SAML%52esponse ahead of the signed SAMLResponse 
    >
    > 🧬 Realm admin privilege escalation  🧬
    > - A help-desk admin maps a pre-built composite of manage-realm onto himself
    > - Same token then reads the full realm document. 
    > - (Undisclosed sibling of CVE-2026-94000, which only walked composites on group join)
    >
    > 🔐👀🎭 Exploits: 
    > - https://github.com/abraxas/keycloak-saml-redirect-param-split  SAML account takeover
    > - https://github.com/abraxas/keycloak-manage-users-composite  auth leftover
    >
    > 🔐👀🎭 Write-ups: 
    > - https://abraxaslabs.tech/research/keycloak-saml-redirect-param-split
    > - https://abraxaslabs.tech/research/keycloak-manage-users-composite
    
    **Media:**
    - photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/HWLYTlJaQAAFvTw.jpg — no alt text provided by author
    
    **Links in post:** https://github.com/abraxas/keycloak-saml-redirect-param-split, https://github.com/abraxas/keycloak-manage-users-composite, https://abraxaslabs.tech/research/keycloak-saml-redirect-param-split, https://abraxaslabs.tech/research/keycloak-manage-users-composite

## 2026-07-29 · Post · YSoNet v2026.7.9 is out 🎉


Tags (automatic): RCE


### Post — @irsdl — 2026-07-29 19:08 UTC (2026-07-29 20:08 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/2082543892352704990
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> YSoNet v2026.7.9 is out 🎉
>
> 31 → 50 gadgets
> 54 → 88 variants
> 118 → 235 gadget/serializer combinations across 17 serializers
>
> 🆕 NEW GADGETS WORTH KNOWING
>
> ▸ DataTable + DataTableTypeSpoof
>
> Same-graph DataTable root carrier for RCE. No nested BinaryFormatter or extra binder boundary. TypeSpoof emits a real DataTable subclass, so blocking only the exact DataTable wire name may not prevent reconstruction.
>
> ▸ TypeConfuseDelegateFileOperations
>
> Five delegate-confusion variants for writing, copying, moving and truncating files. No child process, compiler or WPF required.
>
> ▸ AssemblyInstallerLoad
>
> Loads a specified DLL from a local or UNC path and instantiates [RunInstaller(true)] installer types. Supported by nine serializers.
>
> ▸ DynamicUpdateMapExtension
>
> Passes x:XData to NetDataContractSerializer without a binder, allowing a XAML-only sink to carry an NDCS gadget.
>
> 🔧 NOTABLE UPDATES
>
> ▸ TypeConfuseDelegate now supports SortedDictionary and TreeSet roots, useful for testing blocklists that match only the exact SortedSet wire name.
>
> ▸ WindowsIdentity and WindowsClaimsIdentity can carry the inner BinaryFormatter graph through actor, bootstrapContext, claims or WIF’s _actor sink. Variant numbering is now aligned across both gadgets and all serializers.
>
> 🔗 https://github.com/irsdl/ysonet
>
> http://ysonet.com | http://ysonet.net
>
> #AppSec #YSoNet

## 2026-04-11 · Post · I believe we are still in early phase of AI. I think certain bug classes will be easy to find using 


Tags (automatic): AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @irsdl — 2026-04-11 20:00 UTC (2026-04-11 21:00 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/2043056657253409280
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> I believe we are still in early phase of AI. I think certain bug classes will be easy to find using AI when there is source code but might be hard in dynamic blackbox test; of course AI can be better and faster in recon but it is not limitless.
>
> Bugs coming from new classes or obscure sources or legacy tech or libraries remain too. AI can be used to discover those though new bug classes though.
>
> AI will get better to handle large code bases or at dynamic testing. Combination of sast and dast using code, implementation, build process, and threat models is something that requires building even with AI. Whoever can make this better can play the game better. Using different implementations, we can play it differently!
>
> Just like humans, the limit will be time and money and viewpoints for now. For sure, AI has changed our jobs.

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/PaulosYibelo/status/2041865536779239503

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @PaulosYibelo — 2026-04-08 13:07 UTC (2026-04-08 14:07 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/PaulosYibelo/status/2041865536779239503
    - Metrics (at fetch): not returned
    
    > For people tweeting "cyber security is dead", are u ok? You think when everyone and everything is about to get hacked and the need for security goes through the roof, you think it's "dead" or "solved"? Bruh
    >
    > what it highlights is that security has always been underresourced, not over. Sure your grandma became as good as a professional attacker by simply promoting an llm and that's, granted a scary base entry. What you might not realize is the real determined researcher type attackers just got 1000x more powerful than before. You no longer need to be 20 cracked researchers to zero click RCE an iPhone, you can be one of those guys who is great at one component to be able to build a full chain yourselves. What the mainstream realm seems to not realize is the people who were in the trenches finding the vulns we always knew where there driving these bots will find more mind boggling and complex vulns than your avg hacker. Always been true, will remain true. Look at Poetic, it used particular architrcute bn different LLMs with awesome scaffolding to get Gemini to be 3x better at ARCAGI2.
    >
    > Hacking is not going anywhere. Hackers gonna hack. We gonna hack everything including the Mythos Preview, and other huge ais.
    >
    > Another important thing to raise, esp for ppl who don't spend their time looking for complex bugs in hyper secure software is, different hackers have always found very very different vulnerabilities. In bug bounty, youd often have situations where after the most talented hackers hacked a program, and being open for years, some completely new guy no one has heard of will show up and RCE the program a million ways. And this happens daily. Sometimes it's because that person knows something the rest of the world doesn't, a quirk they figured how to exploit, perhaps a behavior or a zero day (which bounty programs don't often accept), but oftentimes it has nothing to do with that other than how different that person thinks and approaches problems. Their unique life experience.
    > People who have hacked for decade+ like me KNOW to the core of their heart vulnerabilities have ALWAYS been there in large numbers, and in large variety in every set of "secure" software known to man. We've always known it's a matter of time until we break any target, and picking from this buffet of targets to optimize for our time's ROI... Not bc we didn't think they aren't there, or that "15 year old code" would never be vulnerable. 15-20 year old code is exploited daily by hackers, just look at the Linux kernel or windows. It is not a metric of "impressive" - Bc what there always was is unique skills and minds, but not enough time to deploy said x thing into the world in mass, the illusion of being secure has existed. And tbh often pentests and red teams rarely needed new techniques or zero days.
    > These guys who were hacking with their own quirks, who can show up to  mature programs and RCE it a new different way will use the same AI you use to find bugs but find radically different vulns than anything you will find. And there is nothing you can do about it other than cry to your bot. Remember there isn't a finite number of vulns to be found. The chances are there are infinite attack vectors, no I am not exaggerating or using hyperbolic words, it's what I truly believe after hacking for a while. So yes it isn't "solved" by any means, it means you will find ur simple "Claude find me vulns" bugs, and then someone will find something you couldn't even conceptualize, and after all that a bug bounty hunter (or their specialized agent) will show up and still hack you.
    >
    > The need for cyber security innovation (not just bug finding) just went through the roof, not less. Time will show I am right that even after Mythos runs on ur code 20 times, you will be surprised you still got hacked. Someone who thinks hacking away or is just going through  a list of checklist of known vulns has never met a hacker. And it shows!

## 2025-09-18 · Post · 💡 BurpSuite tip (learned it the hard way😅):


### Post — @irsdl — 2025-09-18 08:24 UTC (2025-09-18 09:24 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1968591878812557486
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> 💡 #BurpSuite tip (learned it the hard way😅):
>
> > I assumed I could set different audit rules per target (e.g. chasing specific bugs). ❌ Wrong.
>
> - Unless configs are combined into one rule, Burp only uses the last config. In my screenshot, only the BCheck rule runs!
> All documented here if I had read it: https://web.archive.org/web/20250905143557/https://portswigger.net/burp/documentation/desktop/running-scans/custom-configs
> Missed some bugs → thought it was a Burp bug → asked → turns out it was me 😇
>
> 👉 Upgrade to the latest EA version if you rely on audit to make it less confusing.

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/G1HVlNgX0AAqnQ1.png — alt text: "The old behaviour of Burp Suite only using the last Audit custom config."

**Links in post:** https://web.archive.org/web/20250905143557/https://portswigger.net/burp/documentation/desktop/running-scans/custom-configs

### Post — @irsdl — 2025-07-26 23:28 UTC (2025-07-27 00:28 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1949250528946401635
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 245, impression 36092, like 458, quote 0, reply 4, retweet 122

> I have launched YSoNet (http://ysonet.net) and added #SharePoint CVE-2025-49704 payload generator to it as the first thing.
>
> Here is how this can work:
>
> Running command:
> ```
> ysonet.exe -p sharepoint --cve=CVE-2025-49704 -var 1 -c "calc"
> ```
>
> Running C# code:
>
> ```
> ysonet.exe -p sharepoint --cve=CVE-2025-49704 -var 2 -c "C:\\temp\\ExploitClass.cs;System.dll"
> ```
> Payloads will be url-encoded already.
>
> YSoNet is a fork and replacement of YSoSerial .Net (for me) and I will try to maintain my own version now to have full control over the settings. There are many things I have to change there but all changes will be gradual.
>
> Of course you can still use the great YSoSerial .NET repo but I won't be the one maintaining it. Hopefully I can make @pwntester proud 😊

**Links in post:** http://ysonet.net

## 2025-07-21 · Thread · 🚨


Tags (automatic): Auth bypass, RCE


### Post — @irsdl — 2025-07-21 00:21 UTC (2025-07-21 01:21 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1947089502817706000
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 68, impression 18896, like 156, quote 0, reply 4, retweet 45

> 🚨
> 1- CVE-2025-53770 is a variant of CVE-2025-49704 - a critical auth bypass in SharePoint's ToolPane.aspx endpoint. It lets attackers reach a page that can parse webparts without valid credentials, and with a chained deserialization bug, they can achieve RCE entirely in memory if they wish. #SharePoint
>
> 2- To defenders: block access to ToolPane.aspx in your WAF or IIS. Warning: you may lose custom web part editing until a proper patch arrives. Blocking based on referer is also good but a bit trickier.
>
> 3- What went wrong in Microsoft's auth bypass patch? Two flawed checks:
> A) Path.EndsWith("/ToolPane.aspx") + referer match: bypass with an extra slash (/ToolPane.aspx/)
>
> B) path contains "_layouts" & ends with /ToolPane.aspx: bypass via query string (_layouts/15/ToolPane.aspx/?/ToolPane.aspx)
>
> 4- Even more devious: charset‑encoded URLs can hide the string "/ToolPane.aspx" from WAFs. And HTTP/1.x header folding or leading spaces can break simple referer filters.
>
> 5- If your machine key was auto-generated and exposed, attackers might decrypt/forge viewstate. Rotate those keys: delete old registry entries or recreate the app pool with a new name and assign it to the application.
>
> Microsoft is expected to ship a follow‑up patch. Until then, stay vigilant with URL filtering and header validation. 
> #infosec #cybersecurity
> https://msrc.microsoft.com/update-guide/vulnerability/CVE-2025-53770

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/irsdl/status/1946988895993806913, https://msrc.microsoft.com/update-guide/vulnerability/CVE-2025-53770

    > /_layouts/15/SignOut.aspx

## 2025-07-18 · Post · I originally had Gemini expecting a 200 OK instead of a 401, but after dropping a server-side breakp

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1946166765316161634
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 57, impression 30668, like 139, quote 3, reply 5, retweet 29

> I originally had Gemini expecting a 200 OK instead of a 401, but after dropping a server-side breakpoint so it could use a timeout as the auth signal, it cracked the bypass! 🥈 AI + human teamwork for the win! 🎉
>
> Next: finding the right parameters & deserialization in Toolpane.aspx. No simple patch diffing; only the gadget is clear. First we must identify those params (I know them, but can AI?). Then repurpose the removed gadget into a payload to execute code. We’ll need decompiled Microsoft.PerformancePoint.Scorecards.Client.dll and likely several blog posts. Can AI handle it if we break it into bite-sized tasks? I’m skeptical. GPT models have stumbled on this before - but we’ll see. 🤔
>
> #AI #Reversing #SharePoint
>
> Huge thanks to @_l0gg & @mwulftange for the tips! 🙏

## 2024-08-09 · Thread · I had a CRLF vuln in a HTTP response, it removed characters such as `&lt; &gt; " and =`. 


### Post — @irsdl — 2024-08-09 20:35 UTC (2024-08-09 21:35 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1822008770848792968
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 45, impression 9500, like 90, quote 1, reply 6, retweet 7

> I had a CRLF vuln in a HTTP response, it removed characters such as `&lt; &gt; " and =`. 
> I exploited it by adding CORS headers to allow cross-site content hijacking!
> I also added a `X-Frame-Options:Foobar` to make the existing one invalid so I could exploit it via an iframe!
> #AppSec


### Thread part 2 — @irsdl — 2024-08-09 20:35 UTC (2024-08-09 21:35 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1822008772623233033
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 11, impression 996, like 16, quote 0, reply 0, retweet 3

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 1140, like 0, quote 1, reply 0, retweet 0

    - URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1822330011572920407
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 11, impression 3482, like 26, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 2
    
    > I apologies for my mistake here regarding the `X-Frame-Options` as I didn't notice this for some reasons.
    > It is not possible to open a site in a frame even using 
    > `X-Frame-Options: Foobar,SAMEORIGIN`
    > Also, the target site needs to use SameSite=None which is pretty rare these days.
    > I will work to see if I can bypass any of these by a header when I get the chance. Lack of = and ; is really limiting here.

## 2024-08-10 · Post · I apologies for my mistake here regarding the `X-Frame-Options` as I didn't notice this for some rea


### Post — @irsdl — 2024-08-10 17:51 UTC (2024-08-10 18:51 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1822330011572920407
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 11, impression 3482, like 26, quote 1, reply 1, retweet 2

> I apologies for my mistake here regarding the `X-Frame-Options` as I didn't notice this for some reasons.
> It is not possible to open a site in a frame even using 
> `X-Frame-Options: Foobar,SAMEORIGIN`
> Also, the target site needs to use SameSite=None which is pretty rare these days.
> I will work to see if I can bypass any of these by a header when I get the chance. Lack of = and ; is really limiting here.

## 2024-01-08 · Post · We normally ignore OPTIONS request as noise, but they can be useful sometimes!


### Post — @irsdl — 2024-01-08 10:36 UTC (2024-01-08 11:36 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1744307172211572992
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> We normally ignore OPTIONS request as noise, but they can be useful sometimes!
>
> Jersey example:
> If you have an API with no documentation, perhaps send an OPTIONS request to it and check `&lt;application` in the response to detect WADL which reveals endpoints/params! 
>
> I used this in https://twitter.com/MasteringBurp/status/1744296796459798892

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/MasteringBurp/status/1744296796459798892

    - URL: https://x.com/1303342222650355712/status/1744296796459798892
    - Metrics (at fetch): not returned
    
    > Bambdas don't need to be complex to be useful.
    >
    > Here's one of my favorite ones, hiding "OPTIONS" requests:
    >
    > return !requestResponse.request().method().equals("OPTIONS");

## 2023-03-27 · Post · ASPNET web form tip when bypassing certain WAF rules using COOKIELESS:


### Post — @irsdl — 2023-03-27 16:27 UTC (2023-03-27 17:27 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/irsdl/status/1640390106312835072
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> #ASPNET web form tip when bypassing certain WAF rules using #COOKIELESS:
>
> ✔️WAF blocks `/admin/main.aspx`
> ✔️WAF uses canonicalization &amp; not case sensitive
>
> Possible bypasses:
> 🍪/admin/(S(X))/main.aspx
> 🍪/admin/Foobar/(S(X))/../(S(X))/main.aspx
> 🍪/(S(X))/admin/(S(X))/main.aspx
